# ATHENA — 119 Teaching Packs Deepened (Wave 06B) v1.0

**Status:** deeper authored drafts; no expert review, learner pilot or approval claimed. Each pack now has a distinct module-specific worked case. Existing resource links are optional discovery leads, not a whitelist.

## BASE-BIZ-003 — Value proposition

**Objective:** Connect a customer problem to a credible outcome and differentiator.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Connect a customer problem to a credible outcome and differentiator.

**Mental model.** Separate customer problem, promised outcome, scope, delivery process, costs, cash timing and evidence of demand. A claim is defensible only if it can be delivered and verified.

**Terms to distinguish.** Write a concise value proposition supported by evidence.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Value proposition.** A learner interviews three fictional users of a study planner. Two struggle to remember assignments; one mainly wants decorative themes. The proposed value proposition should target the repeated, evidenced problem—remembering and prioritising work—and name the outcome without claiming guaranteed grades.

**Step 1.** Name one specific customer and observable problem; avoid “everyone”.
**Step 2.** State the deliverable, exclusions, delivery time and support boundary in plain language.
**Step 3.** List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
**Step 4.** Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.
**Step 5.** Review complaints and actual delivery evidence before changing price or scaling.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Connect a customer problem to a credible outcome and differentiator. The assessment evidence is: State who it serves, what changes and why the claim is credible. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Revenue is not profit; profit is not cash; enquiries are not demand proof; a customer promise must match the actual delivery capacity. Check Bangladesh rules with current official sources and a responsible adult before real operations.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A learner interviews three fictional users of a study planner. Two struggle to remember assignments; one mainly wants decorative themes. The proposed value proposition should target the repeated, evidenced problem—remembering and prioritising work—and name the outcome without claiming guaranteed grades.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Name one specific customer and observable problem; avoid “everyone”.
2. State the deliverable, exclusions, delivery time and support boundary in plain language.
3. List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
4. Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.
5. Review complaints and actual delivery evidence before changing price or scaling.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Build a fictional one-page offer and transaction log. Include one customer question, one scope boundary, one cost, one failed-delivery scenario and a truthful response. No real selling or financial commitments are required.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Connect a customer problem to a credible outcome and differentiator.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Revenue is not profit; profit is not cash; enquiries are not demand proof; a customer promise must match the actual delivery capacity. Check Bangladesh rules with current official sources and a responsible adult before real operations. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Revenue is not profit; profit is not cash; enquiries are not demand proof; a customer promise must match the actual delivery capacity. Check Bangladesh rules with current official sources and a responsible adult before real operations.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** State who it serves, what changes and why the claim is credible.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

### Resource discovery plan
**Search query:** beginner business education Value proposition Connect a customer problem to a credible outcome and differentiator. case study practical worksheet Bangladesh context
- Directly teaches the exact objective at the learner’s entry level
- Contains an explanation or demonstration, not merely a topic mention
- Provides worked examples or observable demonstrations where appropriate
- Includes practice/feedback or can be paired with an exercise
- Accurate, current and authoritative for the claim being taught
- Accessible on a phone and usable with limited bandwidth where possible
- Free/open alternatives preferred, but paid resources may be optional enrichment and never a mandatory dependency
- Bangladesh-specific authority required for local legal, financial, emergency and safety procedures
- The resource’s worked example should resemble this module’s concrete case or explicitly explain how its method transfers to it.
**Fallback:** If the best resource is paywalled, inaccessible, too advanced, device-incompatible or jurisdiction-mismatched, keep its value as optional reference only and find an accessible teaching substitute. Do not lower instructional quality to preserve an existing link.

**Safety/scope:** Use fictional customer profiles and sample transactions; no pressure selling, false claims or handling another person’s money. Use fictional customer profiles and sample transactions; no pressure selling, false claims or handling another person’s money.

## BASE-BIZ-005 — Ethical sales conversations

**Objective:** Understand needs and communicate a fit without manipulation.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Understand needs and communicate a fit without manipulation.

**Mental model.** Separate customer problem, promised outcome, scope, delivery process, costs, cash timing and evidence of demand. A claim is defensible only if it can be delivered and verified.

**Terms to distinguish.** Use discovery questions, summarize needs and disclose limitations.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Ethical sales conversations.** A customer asks whether a digital product will guarantee a result. The seller explains what the product actually includes, who it suits, what it cannot do, the price and the delivery conditions, then accepts “no” without pressure.

**Step 1.** Name one specific customer and observable problem; avoid “everyone”.
**Step 2.** State the deliverable, exclusions, delivery time and support boundary in plain language.
**Step 3.** List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
**Step 4.** Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.
**Step 5.** Review complaints and actual delivery evidence before changing price or scaling.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Understand needs and communicate a fit without manipulation. The assessment evidence is: Customer can explain what is included, excluded and why it may or may not fit. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

## BASE-BIZ-006 — Marketing and positioning

**Objective:** Reach a defined audience with a consistent, truthful message.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Reach a defined audience with a consistent, truthful message.

**Mental model.** Separate customer problem, promised outcome, scope, delivery process, costs, cash timing and evidence of demand. A claim is defensible only if it can be delivered and verified.

**Terms to distinguish.** Choose a channel based on audience behaviour and measure a small experiment.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Marketing and positioning.** A small aquarium shop chooses between “fish for everyone” and a message aimed at first-time aquarium owners who need hardy beginner fish and clear care information. The second message is more specific, but every care/availability claim must be accurate.

**Step 1.** Name one specific customer and observable problem; avoid “everyone”.
**Step 2.** State the deliverable, exclusions, delivery time and support boundary in plain language.
**Step 3.** List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
**Step 4.** Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.
**Step 5.** Review complaints and actual delivery evidence before changing price or scaling.

The assessment evidence is: Record reach, response and qualified interest without confusing vanity metrics with demand. ## BASE-BIZ-007 — Funnel and measurement

**Objective:** Track a customer’s path from discovery to purchase and repeat use.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Track a customer’s path from discovery to purchase and repeat use.

**Mental model.** Separate customer problem, promised outcome, scope, delivery process, costs, cash timing and evidence of demand. A claim is defensible only if it can be delivered and verified.

**Terms to distinguish.** Define funnel stages and calculate basic conversion rates.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Funnel and measurement.** A fictional post receives 1,000 views, 40 profile visits, 8 enquiries and 2 paid orders. Calculate each stage’s conversion using the previous stage as denominator, label the denominator, and do not call views “customers”.

**Step 1.** Name one specific customer and observable problem; avoid “everyone”.
**Step 2.** State the deliverable, exclusions, delivery time and support boundary in plain language.
**Step 3.** List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
**Step 4.** Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.
**Step 5.** Review complaints and actual delivery evidence before changing price or scaling.

3. List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
4. Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Revenue is not profit; profit is not cash; enquiries are not demand proof; a customer promise must match the actual delivery capacity. Check Bangladesh rules with current official sources and a responsible adult before real operations.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** A second person could follow the process and recover from common failures.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

## BASE-BIZ-011 — Customer support and retention

**Objective:** Resolve issues while preserving trust and learning from recurring failures.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Resolve issues while preserving trust and learning from recurring failures.

**Mental model.** Separate customer problem, promised outcome, scope, delivery process, costs, cash timing and evidence of demand. A claim is defensible only if it can be delivered and verified.

**Terms to distinguish.** Use a support record, triage and fair resolution policy.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Customer support and retention.** A buyer reports that a delivered file will not open on their phone. The support response acknowledges the issue, asks for the minimum non-sensitive diagnostic detail, provides a safe alternative format and records the root cause for prevention.

**Step 1.** Name one specific customer and observable problem; avoid “everyone”.
**Step 2.** State the deliverable, exclusions, delivery time and support boundary in plain language.
**Step 3.** List variable costs, fixed costs, payment timing and failure/refund scenarios.
**Step 4.** Test the offer with a small, honest sample; distinguish attention, enquiry, order, payment and successful delivery.
**Step 5.** Review complaints and actual delivery evidence before changing price or scaling.

The assessment evidence is: Response is accurate, respectful and consistent with published policy. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

## BASE-ELEC-003 — Measurement instruments

**Objective:** Measure voltage and continuity using correct meter settings and connection.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Measure voltage and continuity using correct meter settings and connection.

**Mental model.** Use circuit diagrams and component models to predict behaviour before any build. Track polarity, current path, voltage, ratings and measurement connections; a correct calculation does not establish physical safety.

**Terms to distinguish.** Explain why current and voltage measurements use different connections.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Measurement instruments.** A multimeter is used in a paper/simulation exercise: voltage is measured across a component, while current is measured in series. Continuity is checked only when the circuit is unpowered. The learner identifies mode and connection before interpreting a reading.

**Step 1.** Redraw the circuit as nodes and connections; mark polarity and current path.
**Step 2.** Write known values with units and identify what is unknown.
**Step 3.** Choose the relevant relationship (for example V=IR) and state its assumptions.
**Step 4.** Calculate and check units, magnitude, component limits and a changed condition.
**Step 5.** Keep the work conceptual/simulated unless a qualified adult approves a suitable isolated low-voltage kit.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Measure voltage and continuity using correct meter settings and connection. The assessment evidence is: Select correct mode and range before connection; record uncertainty. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Never infer safety from low voltage alone. Verify isolation, current limit, polarity, component ratings and instrument connection. Current measurement is in series; voltage measurement is across the element. Continuity mode is used only on an unpowered circuit.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A multimeter is used in a paper/simulation exercise: voltage is measured across a component, while current is measured in series. Continuity is checked only when the circuit is unpowered. The learner identifies mode and connection before interpreting a reading.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Redraw the circuit as nodes and connections; mark polarity and current path.
2. Write known values with units and identify what is unknown.
3. Choose the relevant relationship (for example V=IR) and state its assumptions.
4. Calculate and check units, magnitude, component limits and a changed condition.
5. Keep the work conceptual/simulated unless a qualified adult approves a suitable isolated low-voltage kit.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a circuit simulator or paper diagram only. Predict the current path and voltage relationships, then change one component/value and explain what should change. Do not probe household wiring, mains, chargers or adapters.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

Current measurement is in series; voltage measurement is across the element. Continuity mode is used only on an unpowered circuit. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A low-voltage transistor switch is studied from a schematic and datasheet. The learner identifies control input, load path, common reference and required base/gate drive conceptually, then predicts ON/OFF states in a simulator without building an unreviewed circuit.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Redraw the circuit as nodes and connections; mark polarity and current path.
2. Write known values with units and identify what is unknown.
3. Choose the relevant relationship (for example V=IR) and state its assumptions.
4. Calculate and check units, magnitude, component limits and a changed condition.
5. Keep the work conceptual/simulated unless a qualified adult approves a suitable isolated low-voltage kit.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a circuit simulator or paper diagram only. Predict the current path and voltage relationships, then change one component/value and explain what should change. Do not probe household wiring, mains, chargers or adapters.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Use a transistor as a controlled switch in a conceptual low-voltage circuit.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Never infer safety from low voltage alone. Verify isolation, current limit, polarity, component ratings and instrument connection. Current measurement is in series; voltage measurement is across the element. Continuity mode is used only on an unpowered circuit. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Never infer safety from low voltage alone. Verify isolation, current limit, polarity, component ratings and instrument connection. Current measurement is in series; voltage measurement is across the element. Continuity mode is used only on an unpowered circuit.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Identify control and load paths and component limits.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-ELEC-011 — Breadboard prototyping

## BASE-FIN-007 — Financial statements

**Objective:** Interpret an income statement, balance sheet and cash-flow statement together.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Interpret an income statement, balance sheet and cash-flow statement together.

**Mental model.** Every financial calculation needs amount, timing, units, assumptions and risk. Distinguish a mathematical model from a forecast or recommendation; use fictional examples and Bangladesh-specific official sources for local rules.

**Terms to distinguish.** Explain what each statement can and cannot reveal.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Financial statements.** A fictional shop has sales of ৳5,000, cost of goods sold ৳2,000, operating expenses ৳1,000, cash of ৳1,500, inventory of ৳800 and a supplier payable of ৳600. Identify which figures belong on the income statement, balance sheet or cash-flow statement; do not confuse profit with cash balance.

**Step 1.** Write the question in plain language and identify the time period.
**Step 2.** Separate principal, cash flows, rate, compounding frequency, fees and inflation where relevant.
**Step 3.** Choose a formula/model and state assumptions before calculating.
**Step 4.** Check units, compare a second scenario and test what happens if an assumption changes.
**Step 5.** State uncertainty, downside and what information is missing; do not turn a model into a recommendation.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Interpret an income statement, balance sheet and cash-flow statement together. The assessment evidence is: Conclusion references multiple statements and avoids unsupported certainty. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Nominal gain is not necessarily real purchasing-power gain. Past performance is not a guarantee. Diversification reduces some concentration risks but cannot eliminate loss. Real financial actions require guardian/qualified guidance and current Bangladesh rules.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A fictional shop has sales of ৳5,000, cost of goods sold ৳2,000, operating expenses ৳1,000, cash of ৳1,500, inventory of ৳800 and a supplier payable of ৳600. **Guided sequence.**
1. Write the question in plain language and identify the time period.
2. Separate principal, cash flows, rate, compounding frequency, fees and inflation where relevant.
3. Choose a formula/model and state assumptions before calculating.
4. Check units, compare a second scenario and test what happens if an assumption changes.
5. State uncertainty, downside and what information is missing; do not turn a model into a recommendation.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Solve a fictional money scenario using two cases (base and changed assumption). Show the calculation, units, timeline and one risk/limitation. Do not open accounts, trade, borrow or invest as part of the lesson.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

Real financial actions require guardian/qualified guidance and current Bangladesh rules. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

## BASE-FIN-011 — Fraud and consumer protection

**Objective:** Recognize common financial deception and verify claims before acting.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Recognize common financial deception and verify claims before acting.

**Mental model.** Every financial calculation needs amount, timing, units, assumptions and risk. Distinguish a mathematical model from a forecast or recommendation; use fictional examples and Bangladesh-specific official sources for local rules.

**Terms to distinguish.** Check licensing, custody, withdrawal conditions and unrealistic guarantees.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Fraud and consumer protection.** A message promises a guaranteed high return and asks for an upfront “unlocking fee” through an unknown wallet. Identify urgency, guaranteed-return language, unverifiable identity and payment irreversibility as warning signs; verify independently and involve a guardian before any action.

**Step 1.** Write the question in plain language and identify the time period.
**Step 2.** Separate principal, cash flows, rate, compounding frequency, fees and inflation where relevant.
**Step 3.** Choose a formula/model and state assumptions before calculating.
**Step 4.** Check units, compare a second scenario and test what happens if an assumption changes.
**Step 5.** State uncertainty, downside and what information is missing; do not turn a model into a recommendation.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Recognize common financial deception and verify claims before acting. The assessment evidence is: Lists independent verification steps and refuses urgency-based pressure. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Nominal gain is not necessarily real purchasing-power gain. Past performance is not a guarantee. Diversification reduces some concentration risks but cannot eliminate loss. Real financial actions require guardian/qualified guidance and current Bangladesh rules.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A message promises a guaranteed high return and asks for an upfront “unlocking fee” through an unknown wallet. Identify urgency, guaranteed-return language, unverifiable identity and payment irreversibility as warning signs; verify independently and involve a guardian before any action.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Write the question in plain language and identify the time period.
2. Separate principal, cash flows, rate, compounding frequency, fees and inflation where relevant.
3. Choose a formula/model and state assumptions before calculating.
4. Check units, compare a second scenario and test what happens if an assumption changes.
5. State uncertainty, downside and what information is missing; do not turn a model into a recommendation.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Solve a fictional money scenario using two cases (base and changed assumption). Show the calculation, units, timeline and one risk/limitation. Do not open accounts, trade, borrow or invest as part of the lesson.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Recognize common financial deception and verify claims before acting.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Nominal gain is not necessarily real purchasing-power gain. Past performance is not a guarantee. Diversification reduces some concentration risks but cannot eliminate loss. Real financial actions require guardian/qualified guidance and current Bangladesh rules. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Nominal gain is not necessarily real purchasing-power gain. Past performance is not a guarantee. Diversification reduces some concentration risks but cannot eliminate loss. Real financial actions require guardian/qualified guidance and current Bangladesh rules.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Lists independent verification steps and refuses urgency-based pressure.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-BODY-003 — Anatomical orientation

**Objective:** Use anatomical position, planes, directions and regions accurately.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Use anatomical position, planes, directions and regions accurately.

**Mental model.** Build a causal model from structure → process → observable effect, then identify feedback and limits. A simplified model explains general physiology; it does not diagnose an individual.

**Terms to distinguish.** Describe a structure’s location without relying on viewpoint ambiguity.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Anatomical orientation.** On a neutral anatomical diagram, identify anterior/posterior, superior/inferior and medial/lateral. For example, the sternum is anterior to the heart and the nose is medial to the eyes; orientation is defined from the standard anatomical position.

**Step 1.** Name the structure(s) and the function each contributes.
**Step 2.** Trace the process in order using cause-and-effect arrows.
**Step 3.** Identify the variable being regulated and the feedback response.
**Step 4.** Change one condition (environment, activity or system component) and predict the direction of change.
**Step 5.** Separate general physiology from an individual health conclusion; state when a professional is needed.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Use anatomical position, planes, directions and regions accurately. The assessment evidence is: Terminology is consistent from the subject’s anatomical perspective. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Do not treat averages as an individual prediction or a simplified model as diagnosis/treatment. Avoid diet, supplement or exercise prescriptions; personal health decisions require a qualified professional and trusted adult.

## BASE-BODY-011 — Health information literacy

**Objective:** Evaluate health claims using evidence quality and uncertainty.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Evaluate health claims using evidence quality and uncertainty.

**Mental model.** Build a causal model from structure → process → observable effect, then identify feedback and limits. A simplified model explains general physiology; it does not diagnose an individual.

**Terms to distinguish.** Distinguish general education from individual diagnosis.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Health information literacy.** A social post claims one food “cures” a condition. Check who made the claim, what evidence is cited, whether the source sells the product, what population was studied and whether a qualified health authority supports it.

**Step 1.** Name the structure(s) and the function each contributes.
**Step 2.** Trace the process in order using cause-and-effect arrows.
**Step 3.** Identify the variable being regulated and the feedback response.
**Step 4.** Change one condition (environment, activity or system component) and predict the direction of change.
**Step 5.** Separate general physiology from an individual health conclusion; state when a professional is needed.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Evaluate health claims using evidence quality and uncertainty. The assessment evidence is: Identifies limits and refers personal medical concerns to qualified professionals. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Do not treat averages as an individual prediction or a simplified model as diagnosis/treatment. Avoid diet, supplement or exercise prescriptions; personal health decisions require a qualified professional and trusted adult.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A social post claims one food “cures” a condition. Check who made the claim, what evidence is cited, whether the source sells the product, what population was studied and whether a qualified health authority supports it.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Name the structure(s) and the function each contributes.
2. Trace the process in order using cause-and-effect arrows.
3. Identify the variable being regulated and the feedback response.
4. Change one condition (environment, activity or system component) and predict the direction of change.
5. Separate general physiology from an individual health conclusion; state when a professional is needed.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Draw a labelled flow diagram for the module outcome. Add one changed condition and annotate which links in the model change. Use textbook-level educational cases, not personal symptom interpretation.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Evaluate health claims using evidence quality and uncertainty.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

Avoid diet, supplement or exercise prescriptions; personal health decisions require a qualified professional and trusted adult. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Step 2.** Identify the psychological concept that could explain it and define the concept.
**Step 3.** Generate at least one alternative explanation.
**Step 4.** Ask what evidence would distinguish the explanations; avoid causal claims from a single observation.
**Step 5.** State the limits and apply the idea without diagnosing or manipulating a real person.

## BASE-AEST-006 — Layout systems

**Objective:** Use grids and spacing rules to make layouts consistent.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Use grids and spacing rules to make layouts consistent.

**Mental model.** Design decisions should be tied to a user, task and measurable perceptual/interaction outcome. Evaluate hierarchy, contrast, consistency and accessibility—not personal taste alone.

**Terms to distinguish.** Create responsive layout logic for narrow and wide screens.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Layout systems.** A mobile page contains a title, product image, price, description and action button. A simple grid aligns repeated elements and spacing tokens create rhythm; test at narrow width to ensure the action remains visible.

**Step 1.** Define the user, context and task before choosing visual treatment.
**Step 2.** Identify the one most important action or information relationship.
**Step 3.** Choose the relevant design variables (contrast, type, spacing, alignment, state or composition).
**Step 4.** Create a first version and inspect it at the actual target size/device.
**Step 5.** Test with a task or criterion; revise one variable at a time and check accessibility.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Use grids and spacing rules to make layouts consistent. The assessment evidence is: Content order and touch targets remain usable on small screens. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** A visually attractive design can still be unreadable, inaccessible or misleading. Check contrast, text size, state feedback, touch targets and whether colour is the only signal.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A mobile page contains a title, product image, price, description and action button. A simple grid aligns repeated elements and spacing tokens create rhythm; test at narrow width to ensure the action remains visible.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Define the user, context and task before choosing visual treatment.
2. Identify the one most important action or information relationship.
3. Choose the relevant design variables (contrast, type, spacing, alignment, state or composition).
4. Create a first version and inspect it at the actual target size/device.
5. Test with a task or criterion; revise one variable at a time and check accessibility.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Create two variants of a small interface/poster/layout. State the audience and task, identify the design change, and compare them against explicit criteria such as legibility, hierarchy, contrast and consistency.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Worked case — Brand foundations.** A small repair service chooses a promise of “clear diagnosis before repair”. Its colours, tone, service description and confirmation message should reinforce that promise rather than imply capabilities it does not have.

**Step 1.** Define the user, context and task before choosing visual treatment.
**Step 2.** Identify the one most important action or information relationship.
**Step 3.** Choose the relevant design variables (contrast, type, spacing, alignment, state or composition).
**Step 4.** Create a first version and inspect it at the actual target size/device.
**Step 5.** Test with a task or criterion; revise one variable at a time and check accessibility.

The assessment evidence is: Visual choices support a defined audience and promise. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Task.** Create two variants of a small interface/poster/layout. State the audience and task, identify the design change, and compare them against explicit criteria such as legibility, hierarchy, contrast and consistency.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Create reusable tokens and components with consistent states.

## BASE-WORK-003 — Task capture and triage

**Objective:** Convert open loops into clear next actions.

### Learner explanation

**Mental model.** A workflow is a sequence of observable actions with inputs, outputs, dependencies and failure states. Improve the process only after mapping and measuring what happens now.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Task capture and triage.** Five tasks arrive at once: an urgent school deadline, a routine message, a long-term project and two optional ideas. Capture them first, then sort by deadline, consequence and next action rather than responding to the loudest notification.

**Step 1.** Describe the current process and its desired output.
**Step 2.** Map actions, dependencies, tools, handoffs and failure points.
**Step 3.** Measure a small baseline (time, errors, waiting or rework).
**Step 4.** Change one step; test on copies or a reversible sample.
**Step 5.** Compare the result with the baseline and document rollback/recovery.

## BASE-ATH-003 — Mobility and flexibility

**Objective:** Distinguish joint mobility, flexibility and control.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Distinguish joint mobility, flexibility and control.

**Mental model.** Physical adaptation depends on an appropriate stimulus, technique, recovery and individual context. For young learners, prioritise movement quality, enjoyment, gradual progression and adequate rest.

**Terms to distinguish.** Use gentle range-of-motion practice without forcing end range.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Mobility and flexibility.** A warm-up includes comfortable ankle and hip movements. The learner compares range before and after gentle movement without forcing end range or treating flexibility as a contest.

**Step 1.** Define the performance quality and one observable measure.
**Step 2.** Check readiness, surface, footwear, weather and available recovery.
**Step 3.** Demonstrate the movement at low intensity before increasing demand.
**Step 4.** Use a small number of quality repetitions with adequate rest and feedback.
**Step 5.** Review technique and recovery; progress only when the current level is comfortable and controlled.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Distinguish joint mobility, flexibility and control. The assessment evidence is: No bouncing or forced pain; movement remains controlled. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** More volume/intensity is not always better. Stop for pain, dizziness, chest symptoms, unusual breathlessness or heat-illness signs; do not train through symptoms.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A warm-up includes comfortable ankle and hip movements. The learner compares range before and after gentle movement without forcing end range or treating flexibility as a contest.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Define the performance quality and one observable measure.
2. Check readiness, surface, footwear, weather and available recovery.
3. Demonstrate the movement at low intensity before increasing demand.
4. Use a small number of quality repetitions with adequate rest and feedback.
5. Review technique and recovery; progress only when the current level is comfortable and controlled.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a low-risk movement observation or paper training plan. Identify warm-up, technique cue, rest, stop conditions and one way to track quality. Real training should be age-appropriate and coordinated with a trusted adult/coach.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** More volume/intensity is not always better. Stop for pain, dizziness, chest symptoms, unusual breathlessness or heat-illness signs; do not train through symptoms.

## BASE-ATH-005 — Strength foundations

**Objective:** Explain progressive resistance and safe technique.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Explain progressive resistance and safe technique.

**Mental model.** Physical adaptation depends on an appropriate stimulus, technique, recovery and individual context. For young learners, prioritise movement quality, enjoyment, gradual progression and adequate rest.

**Terms to distinguish.** Choose manageable resistance and leave room for recovery.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Strength foundations.** A bodyweight squat is broken into stance, controlled descent, stable foot contact and comfortable return. The learner uses a manageable range and stops if pain or technique breakdown occurs.

**Step 1.** Define the performance quality and one observable measure.
**Step 2.** Check readiness, surface, footwear, weather and available recovery.
**Step 3.** Demonstrate the movement at low intensity before increasing demand.
**Step 4.** Use a small number of quality repetitions with adequate rest and feedback.
**Step 5.** Review technique and recovery; progress only when the current level is comfortable and controlled.

The assessment evidence is: Technique remains consistent and training is age-appropriate. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a low-risk movement observation or paper training plan. Identify warm-up, technique cue, rest, stop conditions and one way to track quality. Real training should be age-appropriate and coordinated with a trusted adult/coach.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Build a balanced week around goals, constraints and recovery.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** More volume/intensity is not always better. Stop for pain, dizziness, chest symptoms, unusual breathlessness or heat-illness signs; do not train through symptoms. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** More volume/intensity is not always better. Stop for pain, dizziness, chest symptoms, unusual breathlessness or heat-illness signs; do not train through symptoms.

**Performance task:** No abrupt load spikes; plan is realistic and recoverable.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. **Step 2.** Check readiness, surface, footwear, weather and available recovery.

## BASE-PERF-004 — Rhythm and timing

**Objective:** Maintain a pulse and subdivide simple patterns.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Maintain a pulse and subdivide simple patterns.

**Mental model.** Treat performance as a perceptual-motor skill: listen, notice one controllable feature, practise comfortably, record feedback and adjust. Do not equate strain or volume with progress.

**Terms to distinguish.** Count beats and perform simple rhythmic patterns.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Rhythm and timing.** A learner taps a steady four-beat pulse, then speaks “1-and-2-and” evenly over it. They compare the subdivision to the pulse and correct drift without increasing speed.

**Step 1.** Choose one narrow target (pitch, pulse, diction, breath phrasing or expression).
**Step 2.** Hear or observe a clear reference and identify the feature to notice.
**Step 3.** Attempt a short, comfortable repetition and record what happened.
**Step 4.** Compare against the reference using one criterion; adjust one variable only.
**Step 5.** Stop if pain, persistent hoarseness, dizziness or breathing difficulty occurs.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Maintain a pulse and subdivide simple patterns. The assessment evidence is: Maintains pulse and can explain subdivision. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Module case to work through.** A learner taps a steady four-beat pulse, then speaks “1-and-2-and” evenly over it. They compare the subdivision to the pulse and correct drift without increasing speed.

3. Attempt a short, comfortable repetition and record what happened.
4. Compare against the reference using one criterion; adjust one variable only.
5. Stop if pain, persistent hoarseness, dizziness or breathing difficulty occurs.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Choose one narrow target (pitch, pulse, diction, breath phrasing or expression).
2. Hear or observe a clear reference and identify the feature to notice.
3. Attempt a short, comfortable repetition and record what happened.
4. Compare against the reference using one criterion; adjust one variable only.
5. Stop if pain, persistent hoarseness, dizziness or breathing difficulty occurs.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a short, comfortable audio or rhythm exercise. Record one attempt, assess it against a single criterion, rest, and make one controlled adjustment. Do not force vocal range or volume.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Shape phrases using breath, emphasis and dynamics.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** A single recording is not a complete diagnosis of technique. Avoid pushing through pain, hoarseness, dizziness or breath difficulty; seek qualified instruction for persistent problems. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** A single recording is not a complete diagnosis of technique. Avoid pushing through pain, hoarseness, dizziness or breath difficulty; seek qualified instruction for persistent problems.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Choices are intentional and words remain intelligible.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-CYBER-003 — Network foundations

**Objective:** Explain how devices address and exchange data.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Explain how devices address and exchange data.

**Mental model.** Security work begins with permission and scope. Identify the asset, trust boundary, possible failure and defensive control; test only on owned systems, synthetic data or explicitly authorised labs.

**Terms to distinguish.** Distinguish local network, router, DNS and IP address.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Network foundations.** A local diagram shows a phone, Wi-Fi router and internet service. Trace a request from device to router to external service and back; distinguish local network connection from internet access.

**Step 1.** Write the authorisation and scope before touching a system.
**Step 2.** Identify assets, identities, data flows and trust boundaries.
**Step 3.** Describe a plausible failure or threat without attempting unauthorised access.
**Step 4.** Choose a defensive control and a safe test using synthetic data.
**Step 5.** Record evidence, expected result, actual result and remediation; clean up the lab.

**Mental model.** Security work begins with permission and scope. Identify the asset, trust boundary, possible failure and defensive control; test only on owned systems, synthetic data or explicitly authorised labs.

**Terms to distinguish.** Define scope, rules of engagement and safe reporting.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Authorized lab practice.** A learner uses a deliberately vulnerable local lab that explicitly grants permission. They record scope, use synthetic accounts, follow the lab objective and reset the environment afterward; the same actions are not transferred to public systems.

**Step 1.** Write the authorisation and scope before touching a system.
**Step 2.** Identify assets, identities, data flows and trust boundaries.
**Step 3.** Describe a plausible failure or threat without attempting unauthorised access.
**Step 4.** Choose a defensive control and a safe test using synthetic data.
**Step 5.** Record evidence, expected result, actual result and remediation; clean up the lab.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Learn security testing only in systems with explicit permission. The assessment evidence is: No third-party targeting; findings are documented and responsibly handled. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** A login screen does not prove authorisation is correct; a backup is not proven until restore is tested. Never test real accounts, public IPs or third-party systems without explicit permission.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A learner uses a deliberately vulnerable local lab that explicitly grants permission. They record scope, use synthetic accounts, follow the lab objective and reset the environment afterward; the same actions are not transferred to public systems.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Write the authorisation and scope before touching a system.
2. Identify assets, identities, data flows and trust boundaries.
3. Describe a plausible failure or threat without attempting unauthorised access.
4. Choose a defensive control and a safe test using synthetic data.
5. Record evidence, expected result, actual result and remediation; clean up the lab.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a toy local environment or paper threat model with fake data. Produce an asset list, one threat path, one mitigation and one verification check. No public scanning, credential collection or third-party testing.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Learn security testing only in systems with explicit permission.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** A login screen does not prove authorisation is correct; a backup is not proven until restore is tested. Never test real accounts, public IPs or third-party systems without explicit permission. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** A login screen does not prove authorisation is correct; a backup is not proven until restore is tested. Never test real accounts, public IPs or third-party systems without explicit permission.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** No third-party targeting; findings are documented and responsibly handled.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-LANG-003 — Vocabulary acquisition

**Objective:** Learn words with context, collocation and retrieval.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Learn words with context, collocation and retrieval.

**Mental model.** Language skill is demonstrated by meaning in context, not word substitution alone. Track register, grammar, vocabulary, pronunciation/listening cues and whether the receiver understood.

**Terms to distinguish.** Record meaning, usage, register and example phrase.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Vocabulary acquisition.** A learner meets a new word in a sentence, infers a possible meaning, checks a learner dictionary and writes a new sentence using it. The word is revisited later in a different context.

**Step 1.** Identify speaker, audience, purpose, register and context.
**Step 2.** Extract the meaning or structure before translating or responding.
**Step 3.** Choose vocabulary/grammar that preserves the relationship between ideas.
**Step 4.** Check comprehension by paraphrase, a task or a second representation.
**Step 5.** Note ambiguity, dialect/register differences and terms that should remain bilingual.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Learn words with context, collocation and retrieval. The assessment evidence is: Can recall and use words appropriately, not just recognize translations. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Word-for-word equivalence can distort meaning. Do not treat one accent, dialect or register as inherently inferior; evaluate intelligibility and task success.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A learner meets a new word in a sentence, infers a possible meaning, checks a learner dictionary and writes a new sentence using it. The word is revisited later in a different context.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Identify speaker, audience, purpose, register and context.
2. Extract the meaning or structure before translating or responding.
3. Choose vocabulary/grammar that preserves the relationship between ideas.
4. Check comprehension by paraphrase, a task or a second representation.
5. Note ambiguity, dialect/register differences and terms that should remain bilingual.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?
