**What you are learning.** Write clear paragraphs with a claim, support and logical sequence.

**Mental model.** Language skill is demonstrated by meaning in context, not word substitution alone. Track register, grammar, vocabulary, pronunciation/listening cues and whether the receiver understood.

**Terms to distinguish.** Revise for audience, coherence and evidence.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Writing foundations.** A paragraph makes one claim, supports it with a concrete example and explains why the example matters. Remove a sentence that does not support the main point and check the logical sequence.

**Step 1.** Identify speaker, audience, purpose, register and context.
**Step 2.** Extract the meaning or structure before translating or responding.
**Step 3.** Choose vocabulary/grammar that preserves the relationship between ideas.
**Step 4.** Check comprehension by paraphrase, a task or a second representation.
**Step 5.** Note ambiguity, dialect/register differences and terms that should remain bilingual.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Write clear paragraphs with a claim, support and logical sequence. The assessment evidence is: Reader can identify main point and supporting reason. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Word-for-word equivalence can distort meaning. Do not treat one accent, dialect or register as inherently inferior; evaluate intelligibility and task success.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A paragraph makes one claim, supports it with a concrete example and explains why the example matters. Remove a sentence that does not support the main point and check the logical sequence.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Identify speaker, audience, purpose, register and context.
2. Extract the meaning or structure before translating or responding.
3. Choose vocabulary/grammar that preserves the relationship between ideas.
4. Check comprehension by paraphrase, a task or a second representation.
5. Note ambiguity, dialect/register differences and terms that should remain bilingual.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Work with a short authentic passage or recording. Produce a paraphrase/translation, underline ambiguous terms, and explain how you checked that the intended meaning survived.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Write clear paragraphs with a claim, support and logical sequence.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Word-for-word equivalence can distort meaning. Do not treat one accent, dialect or register as inherently inferior; evaluate intelligibility and task success. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Word-for-word equivalence can distort meaning. Do not treat one accent, dialect or register as inherently inferior; evaluate intelligibility and task success.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Reader can identify main point and supporting reason.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-RES-005 — Citation and provenance

**Objective:** Record sources so another person can locate and evaluate them.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Record sources so another person can locate and evaluate them.

**Mental model.** Research starts with a question and a claim that could be checked. Methods, sampling, measurement, source independence and uncertainty determine what the evidence can support.

**Terms to distinguish.** Capture author, title, publisher, date, URL/identifier and access details.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Citation and provenance.** A report uses a statistic from a government PDF that itself cites another report. Record the exact page, publisher, date and original source where possible; do not cite a search snippet as if it were the primary evidence.

**Step 1.** Turn the topic into a narrow, answerable question.
**Step 2.** Define key terms, population/sample and what evidence would count.
**Step 3.** Choose a proportionate, ethical method and record it before collecting data.
**Step 4.** Check source quality, measurement error, bias and alternative explanations.
**Step 5.** Report the finding with method, evidence, uncertainty and limits; preserve enough detail to reproduce it.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Record sources so another person can locate and evaluate them. The assessment evidence is: References are complete enough to retrieve and verify. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** More sources do not guarantee stronger evidence if they repeat the same claim. Do not secretly record people, expose private data or make unsupported claims about identifiable individuals.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A report uses a statistic from a government PDF that itself cites another report. Record the exact page, publisher, date and original source where possible; do not cite a search snippet as if it were the primary evidence.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Turn the topic into a narrow, answerable question.
2. Define key terms, population/sample and what evidence would count.
3. Choose a proportionate, ethical method and record it before collecting data.
4. Check source quality, measurement error, bias and alternative explanations.
5. Report the finding with method, evidence, uncertainty and limits; preserve enough detail to reproduce it.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Design a mini research plan using public or synthetic data only. State the question, method, sample limits, privacy protections and what conclusion would be too strong.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Record sources so another person can locate and evaluate them.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** More sources do not guarantee stronger evidence if they repeat the same claim. Do not secretly record people, expose private data or make unsupported claims about identifiable individuals. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** More sources do not guarantee stronger evidence if they repeat the same claim. Do not secretly record people, expose private data or make unsupported claims about identifiable individuals.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** References are complete enough to retrieve and verify.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-RES-011 — Reproducibility

**Objective:** Preserve data, code, methods and decisions needed to repeat work.

## BASE-INDEP-003 — Cooking foundations

**Objective:** Prepare simple meals using safe heat and knife practices.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Prepare simple meals using safe heat and knife practices.

**Mental model.** Practical independence is knowing the safe next step and recognising when not to proceed. Follow product labels and current Bangladesh authority guidance; serious hazards require a responsible adult or trained responder.

**Terms to distinguish.** Follow a recipe and sequence tasks efficiently.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

### Worked example

**Step 5.** Check the result and know the stop/escalation condition.

**Terms to distinguish.** Use simple tools safely on non-electrical, low-risk tasks.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Basic tools and repairs.** A loose screw on a non-electrical household item is identified. The learner matches screwdriver type and size, checks whether the repair is safe and asks an adult before proceeding if the item is structural, electrical or unfamiliar.

**Step 1.** Define the task and identify hazards before acting.
**Step 2.** Read the product/equipment label and current local instructions.
**Step 3.** Prepare a safe environment and the minimum necessary materials.
**Step 4.** Follow one verified procedure without mixing or improvising.
**Step 5.** Check the result and know the stop/escalation condition.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Identify common hand tools and know when not to attempt a repair. The assessment evidence is: Uses correct tool and stops when structural/electrical risk appears. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Confidence is not competence. Never mix cleaning chemicals, attempt unfamiliar electrical/gas repairs, or delay emergency help. First-aid literacy is not practical certification.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A loose screw on a non-electrical household item is identified. The learner matches screwdriver type and size, checks whether the repair is safe and asks an adult before proceeding if the item is structural, electrical or unfamiliar.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Define the task and identify hazards before acting.
2. Read the product/equipment label and current local instructions.
3. Prepare a safe environment and the minimum necessary materials.
4. Follow one verified procedure without mixing or improvising.
5. Check the result and know the stop/escalation condition.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Use a fictional scenario or supervised demonstration plan. Identify hazards, label/source to consult, safe steps, stop condition and who to contact. Do not practise hazardous repairs, chemicals, heat, travel or first aid alone.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Identify common hand tools and know when not to attempt a repair.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Confidence is not competence. Never mix cleaning chemicals, attempt unfamiliar electrical/gas repairs, or delay emergency help. First-aid literacy is not practical certification. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Confidence is not competence. Never mix cleaning chemicals, attempt unfamiliar electrical/gas repairs, or delay emergency help. First-aid literacy is not practical certification.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Uses correct tool and stops when structural/electrical risk appears.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-LEAD-003 — Listening and understanding

**Objective:** Listen for goals, constraints and unspoken ambiguity.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Listen for goals, constraints and unspoken ambiguity.

**Mental model.** Coordination requires a shared outcome, clear roles, decision rights, feedback and a way to surface risk. Leadership is not coercion; responsibility must match authority and support.

**Terms to distinguish.** Paraphrase and ask clarifying questions before proposing solutions.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Listening and understanding.** A teammate says a deadline is difficult because another task is unfinished. The listener paraphrases the concern, asks what dependency is blocking progress and confirms the desired outcome before offering a solution.

**Step 1.** Restate the shared outcome and what “done” means.
**Step 2.** Clarify roles, ownership, decision rights and dependencies.
**Step 3.** Agree on communication cadence and how blockers/risks are raised.
**Step 4.** Use evidence and interests to resolve a disagreement; record the decision.
**Step 5.** Review commitments and adjust support or scope without humiliation or retaliation.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Listen for goals, constraints and unspoken ambiguity. The assessment evidence is: Other person confirms the summary is accurate. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A teammate says a deadline is difficult because another task is unfinished. The listener paraphrases the concern, asks what dependency is blocking progress and confirms the desired outcome before offering a solution.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Restate the shared outcome and what “done” means.
2. Clarify roles, ownership, decision rights and dependencies.
3. Agree on communication cadence and how blockers/risks are raised.
4. Use evidence and interests to resolve a disagreement; record the decision.
5. Review commitments and adjust support or scope without humiliation or retaliation.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Coordinate a fictional small team task. Produce a one-page responsibility map, timeline, risk list and decision record. Include one disagreement and a fair process for resolving it.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Listen for goals, constraints and unspoken ambiguity.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Other person confirms the summary is accurate.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer

**What you are learning.** Translate a broad aim into observable milestones.

**Mental model.** Coordination requires a shared outcome, clear roles, decision rights, feedback and a way to surface risk. Leadership is not coercion; responsibility must match authority and support.

**Terms to distinguish.** Define outcome, leading action and review condition.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Goal setting.** A broad aim to “improve English” becomes an observable milestone: summarise one short article in five sentences and correctly explain three unfamiliar terms each week. The milestone can be checked and adjusted.

**Step 1.** Restate the shared outcome and what “done” means.
**Step 2.** Clarify roles, ownership, decision rights and dependencies.
**Step 3.** Agree on communication cadence and how blockers/risks are raised.
**Step 4.** Use evidence and interests to resolve a disagreement; record the decision.
**Step 5.** Review commitments and adjust support or scope without humiliation or retaliation.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Translate a broad aim into observable milestones. The assessment evidence is: Measures reflect progress rather than vanity. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A broad aim to “improve English” becomes an observable milestone: summarise one short article in five sentences and correctly explain three unfamiliar terms each week. The milestone can be checked and adjusted.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Restate the shared outcome and what “done” means.
2. Clarify roles, ownership, decision rights and dependencies.
3. Agree on communication cadence and how blockers/risks are raised.
4. Use evidence and interests to resolve a disagreement; record the decision.
5. Review commitments and adjust support or scope without humiliation or retaliation.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Coordinate a fictional small team task. Produce a one-page responsibility map, timeline, risk list and decision record. Include one disagreement and a fair process for resolving it.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Translate a broad aim into observable milestones.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Measures reflect progress rather than vanity.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

## BASE-LEAD-010 — Decision and accountability

**Objective:** Make decisions with clear ownership and review.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Make decisions with clear ownership and review.

**Mental model.** Coordination requires a shared outcome, clear roles, decision rights, feedback and a way to surface risk. Leadership is not coercion; responsibility must match authority and support.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Decision and accountability.** A team chooses between two feasible plans. It records criteria, evidence, decision owner, dissenting concern and review date so responsibility and later correction are visible.

**Step 1.** Restate the shared outcome and what “done” means.
**Step 2.** Clarify roles, ownership, decision rights and dependencies.
**Step 3.** Agree on communication cadence and how blockers/risks are raised.
**Step 4.** Use evidence and interests to resolve a disagreement; record the decision.
**Step 5.** Review commitments and adjust support or scope without humiliation or retaliation.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Make decisions with clear ownership and review. The assessment evidence is: Decision can be reviewed without blaming individuals. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** A title does not create unlimited authority. Avoid coercion, humiliation, retaliation or unsafe power imbalance; safeguarding concerns go to a trusted adult.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A team chooses between two feasible plans. It records criteria, evidence, decision owner, dissenting concern and review date so responsibility and later correction are visible.

2. Clarify roles, ownership, decision rights and dependencies.
3. Agree on communication cadence and how blockers/risks are raised.

5. Review commitments and adjust support or scope without humiliation or retaliation.

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

## BASE-COMM-003 — Clarity and concision

**Objective:** Express one main idea with precise wording.

### Learner explanation
**What you are learning.** Express one main idea with precise wording.

**Mental model.** Communication succeeds when the receiver can accurately understand or use the message. Structure claims, reasons, evidence, audience needs and feedback; persuasion must preserve truth and material limitations.

**Terms to distinguish.** Remove ambiguity and unnecessary filler without losing meaning.. These terms are not interchangeable: explain what each contributes to the outcome, then show how they connect in one real example.

**Decision rule.** Before using a method, state the inputs and conditions it assumes. After using it, check the result against the objective and identify what evidence would make you revise the conclusion.

### Worked example
**Worked case — Clarity and concision.** A long update is reduced to the decision needed, two supporting facts and the next action. Remove repeated context while retaining the caveat that changes the decision.

**Step 1.** Identify the audience, purpose and action/inference needed.
**Step 2.** Choose the main point and order supporting information by importance.
**Step 3.** Separate claim, reason, evidence and uncertainty.
**Step 4.** Use an example or structure suited to the audience; remove ambiguity and unsupported certainty.
**Step 5.** Check understanding through a paraphrase, question or task—not merely “does that make sense?”.

**Apply it to this module.** The learner's output must directly demonstrate: Express one main idea with precise wording. The assessment evidence is: Meaning is preserved and reader effort is reduced. A correct answer must show the relevant decisions, not merely repeat the terminology.

**Check the boundary.** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** A long update is reduced to the decision needed, two supporting facts and the next action. Remove repeated context while retaining the caveat that changes the decision.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Identify the audience, purpose and action/inference needed.
2. Choose the main point and order supporting information by importance.
3. Separate claim, reason, evidence and uncertainty.
4. Use an example or structure suited to the audience; remove ambiguity and unsupported certainty.
5. Check understanding through a paraphrase, question or task—not merely “does that make sense?”.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Create a short explanation or message for a defined audience. Ask a reader to paraphrase the main point; revise any part they misunderstood. Include one limitation or uncertainty honestly.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance

**Common correction:** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Meaning is preserved and reader effort is reduced.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

**Check the boundary.** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists.

**Module case to work through.** A learner explains fractions using a pizza diagram, then checks understanding with a new example that is not identical. If the listener cannot solve it, the explanation is revised rather than repeated louder.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Identify the audience, purpose and action/inference needed.
2. Choose the main point and order supporting information by importance.
3. Separate claim, reason, evidence and uncertainty.
4. Use an example or structure suited to the audience; remove ambiguity and unsupported certainty.
5. Check understanding through a paraphrase, question or task—not merely “does that make sense?”.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Create a short explanation or message for a defined audience. Ask a reader to paraphrase the main point; revise any part they misunderstood. Include one limitation or uncertainty honestly.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Make an idea understandable through examples and checks.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Learner can transfer the idea, not merely repeat the wording.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

**Check the boundary.** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists.

### Guided practice
**Module case to work through.** Two people need to schedule shared work time. They identify each person’s constraints, separate positions from interests and record the agreed time, responsibilities and fallback if the plan changes.

**Guided sequence.**
1. Identify the audience, purpose and action/inference needed.
2. Choose the main point and order supporting information by importance.
3. Separate claim, reason, evidence and uncertainty.
4. Use an example or structure suited to the audience; remove ambiguity and unsupported certainty.
5. Check understanding through a paraphrase, question or task—not merely “does that make sense?”.

**Coach prompts.** What is the exact outcome? Which input or condition matters most? What would count as evidence? What could make the conclusion wrong? Which step needs adult/qualified supervision or must remain simulated?

**Feedback rule.** Correct the first consequential error in the reasoning chain, then let the learner retry a similar but non-identical case.

### Independent practice
**Task.** Create a short explanation or message for a defined audience. Ask a reader to paraphrase the main point; revise any part they misunderstood. Include one limitation or uncertainty honestly.

**Variation A — change a condition.** Change one input, audience, context or constraint while keeping the objective fixed. Predict the effect before checking.

**Variation B — diagnose a flawed attempt.** Create or inspect a plausible incorrect solution. Identify the first invalid step, explain why it fails and repair it.

**Evidence to submit.** The artefact/answer, the reasoning, one boundary check and a short statement of what remains uncertain.

### Answer guidance
A demonstrated response must satisfy the exact outcome: Clarify interests and seek agreements with explicit terms.

For the worked case, the reasoning should follow the sequence in the example, use the relevant terms accurately, and connect the decision to the observed result.

**Checkpoints:** (1) correct interpretation of the task; (2) appropriate model/method; (3) accurate execution or explanation; (4) independent verification; (5) meaningful boundary/limitation.

**Common correction:** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists. A response that reaches a plausible result by unsupported guessing is not yet demonstrated.

### Misconceptions and feedback
**Likely misconception 1:** knowing the vocabulary means being able to perform the skill. **Feedback:** ask the learner to produce the observable output required by the objective.

**Likely misconception 2:** one successful example proves the method always works. **Feedback:** use a changed-condition or boundary case.

**Likely misconception 3:** a model is the whole of reality. **Feedback:** ask what the model omits and what evidence would require a different explanation.

**Domain-specific caution:** Sending is not understanding. A confident argument can still be unsound if its premises are weak. Do not invent scarcity, hide fees or imply certainty where none exists.

### Assessment
**Performance task:** Agreement records scope, responsibilities and conditions.

**Submission:** produce the relevant artefact/answer, annotate the key decisions, check one boundary condition and explain one limitation. Where the objective is primarily conceptual, a labelled model plus an accurate explanation is acceptable; where it is procedural, the learner must demonstrate the procedure in a safe setting.

### Retrieval and transfer
Immediately after instruction, reconstruct from memory: (1) the objective; (2) the core model; (3) the steps; (4) one common error; (5) one boundary. Later checks should mix short recall with a fresh application. If recall fails, provide a brief restudy cue and retry; do not treat repeated failure as mastery.

**Near transfer:** apply the same method to a new example in the same domain with one changed input. **Far transfer:** apply the underlying reasoning to a different context and identify what does not transfer. In both cases, explain the shared principle, changed assumptions and new evidence needed.

### Resource discovery plan
**Search query:** beginner communication lesson Negotiation foundations Clarify interests and seek agreements with explicit terms. worked example practice open educational resource
- Directly teaches the exact objective at the learner’s entry level
- Contains an explanation or demonstration, not merely a topic mention
- Provides worked examples or observable demonstrations where appropriate
- Includes practice/feedback or can be paired with an exercise
- Accurate, current and authoritative for the claim being taught
- Accessible on a phone and usable with limited bandwidth where possible
- Free/open alternatives preferred, but paid resources may be optional enrichment and never a mandatory dependency
- Bangladesh-specific authority required for local legal, financial, emergency and safety procedures
- The resource’s worked example should resemble this module’s concrete case or explicitly explain how its method transfers to it.
**Fallback:** If the best resource is paywalled, inaccessible, too advanced, device-incompatible or jurisdiction-mismatched, keep its value as optional reference only and find an accessible teaching substitute. Do not lower instructional quality to preserve an existing link.

**Safety/scope:** No deception, fabricated evidence, concealed material terms or manipulative urgency. No deception, fabricated evidence, concealed material terms or manipulative urgency.
